[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve started arranging my whole house around not seeing spiders. It sounds silly when I say it out loud. I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but I can’t make myself go down to the basement to do laundry if I think there might be one there.\n\nAnd the garden shed—honestly, I used to keep everything organized in there, labels on boxes and all that, which my husband always teased me about. But now I avoid it, or I pay someone to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t face going into those spaces. It’s become… inconvenient, and embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about spiders when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly. I mean, if I’m at work marking papers or dealing with Year 10s being dramatic, I’m not sitting there picturing spiders all day. Though funny enough, the biology teacher has those posters with insects and things—well, not spiders technically, I suppose—and I always find myself not looking too closely.\n\nBut… no, I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts that just pop in all the time. It’s more when I’m about to go somewhere they might be, then my mind goes there. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific when you think about spiders to make yourself feel better or less anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a… ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’ll sometimes just back away or call my husband, but that’s more because I don’t want to deal with it.\n\nMind you, I do check corners sometimes, but then again I’ve always been a bit particular about the house. My mother was the same—she could spot dust on a skirting board from across the room, which used to drive us all mad. So I don’t know whether that’s related or just me being me.\n\nBut no, I can’t think of something specific I feel I have to do in my head to calm down. It’s more that I want to get away from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions when you think about spiders or see them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s an overwhelming urge in that way, no. I mean, if I see one, I’ll want to get away from it, obviously, or get someone else to deal with it, but I don’t have a particular action I feel I must do, like counting or touching something.\n\nAlthough, saying that, I suppose everyone has little habits, don’t they? My husband checks the back door three times before bed, and he insists that’s “just being sensible.” But with spiders, for me, it’s more… panic and wanting distance, not some specific thing I have to perform.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to avoid spiders is more about the spiders themselves or the feeling of disorder they might bring to your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the spiders themselves, honestly. I mean, I do like the house tidy—teaching makes you cling to order where you can, doesn’t it? My classroom cupboard is another story entirely, mind you, piles of worksheets from years ago…\n\nBut no, at home it’s more that I arrange things so I don’t have to see them. I’ll put off doing laundry if it means going down to the basement, and I avoid the garden shed unless I absolutely have to. I’ve even paid people to do silly little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces in case there’s a spider there. So it’s not really disorder, it’s avoiding where I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is related to a fear of the spiders themselves or a fear of something else that might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s the spiders themselves, I think. Not that I genuinely believe every spider is going to do something terrible—if I’m being sensible, I know one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nBut then I picture one dropping out of a cardboard box, or being right there by the washer when I’m reaching in, and suddenly it feels… huge, somehow. Threatening. Which is silly, I know. I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I can deal with teenagers arguing over homework, but a little spider near the laundry and I’m useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking corners is something you need to do to feel safe or in control when you think about spiders?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it needing to feel in control, exactly. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I suppose it’s both, but more specifically I’m trying not to see them. The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.

My husband checks the back door three times before bed, and he insists that’s “just being sensible.” But with spiders, for me, it’s more… panic and wanting distance, not some specific thing I have to perform.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to avoid spiders is more about the spiders themselves or the feeling of disorder they might bring to your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the spiders themselves, honestly. So it’s not really disorder, it’s avoiding where I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is related to a fear of the spiders themselves or a fear of something else that might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s the spiders themselves, I think. Not that I genuinely believe every spider is going to do something terrible—if I’m being sensible, I know one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nBut then I picture one dropping out of a cardboard box, or being right there by the washer when I’m reaching in, and suddenly it feels… huge, somehow. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Yes, I think if I felt I couldn’t control whether they might be there, I’d start avoiding it. It’s not even always that I’ve seen one — it’s the possibility.

Sorry, I’m going round the houses a bit, but yes — if I can’t feel sure there won’t be spiders, I tend to avoid the place.

Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific when you think about spiders to make yourself feel better or less anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a… ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’ll sometimes just back away or call my husband, but that’s more because I don’t want to deal with it.\n\nMind you, I do check corners sometimes, but then again I’ve always been a bit particular about the house. My mother was the same—she could spot dust on a skirting board from across the room, which used to drive us all mad. So I don’t know whether that’s related or just me being me.\n\nBut no, I can’t think of something specific I feel I have to do in my head to calm down. It’s more that I want to get away from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions when you think about spiders or see them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s an overwhelming urge in that way, no. I mean, if I see one, I’ll want to get away from it, obviously, or get someone else to deal with it, but I don’t have a particular action I feel I must do, like counting or touching something.\n\nAlthough, saying that, I suppose everyone has little habits, don’t they? My husband checks the back door three times before bed, and he insists that’s “just being sensible.” But with spiders, for me, it’s more… panic and wanting distance, not some specific thing I have to perform.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to avoid spiders is more about the spiders themselves or the feeling of disorder they might bring to your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the spiders themselves, honestly. I mean, I do like the house tidy—teaching makes you cling to order where you can, doesn’t it? My classroom cupboard is another story entirely, mind you, piles of worksheets from years ago…\n\nBut no, at home it’s more that I arrange things so I don’t have to see them. I’ll put off doing laundry if it means going down to the basement, and I avoid the garden shed unless I absolutely have to. I’ve even paid people to do silly little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces in case there’s a spider there. So it’s not really disorder, it’s avoiding where I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is related to a fear of the spiders themselves or a fear of something else that might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s the spiders themselves, I think. Not that I genuinely believe every spider is going to do something terrible—if I’m being sensible, I know one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nBut then I picture one dropping out of a cardboard box, or being right there by the washer when I’m reaching in, and suddenly it feels… huge, somehow. Threatening. Which is silly, I know. I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I can deal with teenagers arguing over homework, but a little spider near the laundry and I’m useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking corners is something you need to do to feel safe or in control when you think about spiders?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it needing to feel in control, exactly. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding spiders more about reducing your anxiety or specifically about not wanting to see the spiders themselves?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but more specifically I’m trying not to see them. The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.\n\nI mean, it sounds daft when I say it out loud. I’ve got piles of laundry I’ll put off because the washer’s in the basement, and then I’ll busy myself with marking or cleaning the kitchen instead, as if that’s more urgent. And the garden shed… I just avoid it. I’ve even paid people to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just so I don’t have to go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to avoid other places or situations if you couldn't control whether or not spiders were present, even if spiders weren't actually there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think if I felt I couldn’t control whether they might be there, I’d start avoiding it. It’s not even always that I’ve seen one — it’s the possibility.\n\nThat’s really what’s been happening at home. I’ve got my own little routes and habits now, which sounds absurd. The basement, for instance, because the washer’s down there, I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous. And the garden shed — I’ll need something from it and then decide I don’t really need it after all. I’ve even paid someone to do small jobs I could do myself, just because it meant I didn’t have to go into those spaces.\n\nSorry, I’m going round the houses a bit, but yes — if I can’t feel sure there won’t be spiders, I tend to avoid the place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.\n\nI mean, it sounds daft when I say it out loud. I’ve got piles of laundry I’ll put off because the washer’s in the basement, and then I’ll busy myself with marking or cleaning the kitchen instead, as if that’s more urgent. And the garden shed… I just avoid it. I’ve even paid people to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just so I don’t have to go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to avoid other places or situations if you couldn't control whether or not spiders were present, even if spiders weren't actually there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think if I felt I couldn’t control whether they might be there, I’d start avoiding it. It’s not even always that I’ve seen one — it’s the possibility.\n\nThat’s really what’s been happening at home. I’ve got my own little routes and habits now, which sounds absurd. The basement, for instance, because the washer’s down there, I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous. And the garden shed — I’ll need something from it and then decide I don’t really need it after all. I’ve even paid someone to do small jobs I could do myself, just because it meant I didn’t have to go into those spaces.\n\nSorry, I’m going round the houses a bit, but yes — if I can’t feel sure there won’t be spiders, I tend to avoid the place.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still avoid those places, like the basement or the garden shed, if you were accompanied by someone you trust?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a bit less, if someone was with me… but I think I’d still try to get out of it, honestly. I’ve even had times where my husband says, “I’ll come down with you,” and then I find some reason why it can wait.

It’s silly, because I know the basement is just the basement, and the shed is just full of old paint tins and garden tools and all that clutter you swear you’ll sort one summer and never do. But what brings me here, really, is that I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I put off laundry because the machine’s down there, I avoid the garden shed, and I’ve paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.

So yes, someone being there might help me endure it for a minute, but it doesn’t really stop me wanting to avoid it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve started arranging my whole house around not seeing spiders. It sounds silly when I say it out loud. I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but I can’t make myself go down to the basement to do laundry if I think there might be one there.\n\nAnd the garden shed—honestly, I used to keep everything organized in there, labels on boxes and all that, which my husband always teased me about. But now I avoid it, or I pay someone to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t face going into those spaces. It’s become… inconvenient, and embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about spiders when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly. I mean, if I’m at work marking papers or dealing with Year 10s being dramatic, I’m not sitting there picturing spiders all day. Though funny enough, the biology teacher has those posters with insects and things—well, not spiders technically, I suppose—and I always find myself not looking too closely.\n\nBut… no, I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts that just pop in all the time. It’s more when I’m about to go somewhere they might be, then my mind goes there. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific when you think about spiders to make yourself feel better or less anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a… ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’ll sometimes just back away or call my husband, but that’s more because I don’t want to deal with it.\n\nMind you, I do check corners sometimes, but then again I’ve always been a bit particular about the house. My mother was the same—she could spot dust on a skirting board from across the room, which used to drive us all mad. So I don’t know whether that’s related or just me being me.\n\nBut no, I can’t think of something specific I feel I have to do in my head to calm down. It’s more that I want to get away from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions when you think about spiders or see them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s an overwhelming urge in that way, no. I mean, if I see one, I’ll want to get away from it, obviously, or get someone else to deal with it, but I don’t have a particular action I feel I must do, like counting or touching something.\n\nAlthough, saying that, I suppose everyone has little habits, don’t they? My husband checks the back door three times before bed, and he insists that’s “just being sensible.” But with spiders, for me, it’s more… panic and wanting distance, not some specific thing I have to perform.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to avoid spiders is more about the spiders themselves or the feeling of disorder they might bring to your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the spiders themselves, honestly. I mean, I do like the house tidy—teaching makes you cling to order where you can, doesn’t it? My classroom cupboard is another story entirely, mind you, piles of worksheets from years ago…\n\nBut no, at home it’s more that I arrange things so I don’t have to see them. I’ll put off doing laundry if it means going down to the basement, and I avoid the garden shed unless I absolutely have to. I’ve even paid people to do silly little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces in case there’s a spider there. So it’s not really disorder, it’s avoiding where I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is related to a fear of the spiders themselves or a fear of something else that might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s the spiders themselves, I think. Not that I genuinely believe every spider is going to do something terrible—if I’m being sensible, I know one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nBut then I picture one dropping out of a cardboard box, or being right there by the washer when I’m reaching in, and suddenly it feels… huge, somehow. Threatening. Which is silly, I know. I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I can deal with teenagers arguing over homework, but a little spider near the laundry and I’m useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking corners is something you need to do to feel safe or in control when you think about spiders?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it needing to feel in control, exactly. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding spiders more about reducing your anxiety or specifically about not wanting to see the spiders themselves?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but more specifically I’m trying not to see them. The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.\n\nI mean, it sounds daft when I say it out loud. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding spiders more about reducing your anxiety or specifically about not wanting to see the spiders themselves?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but more specifically I’m trying not to see them. The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.\n\nI mean, it sounds daft when I say it out loud. I’ve got piles of laundry I’ll put off because the washer’s in the basement, and then I’ll busy myself with marking or cleaning the kitchen instead, as if that’s more urgent. And the garden shed… I just avoid it. I’ve even paid people to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just so I don’t have to go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to avoid other places or situations if you couldn't control whether or not spiders were present, even if spiders weren't actually there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think if I felt I couldn’t control whether they might be there, I’d start avoiding it. It’s not even always that I’ve seen one — it’s the possibility.\n\nThat’s really what’s been happening at home. I’ve got my own little routes and habits now, which sounds absurd. The basement, for instance, because the washer’s down there, I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous. And the garden shed — I’ll need something from it and then decide I don’t really need it after all. I’ve even paid someone to do small jobs I could do myself, just because it meant I didn’t have to go into those spaces.\n\nSorry, I’m going round the houses a bit, but yes — if I can’t feel sure there won’t be spiders, I tend to avoid the place.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid those places, like the basement or the garden shed, if you were accompanied by someone you trust?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit less, if someone was with me… but I think I’d still try to get out of it, honestly. I’ve even had times where my husband says, “I’ll come down with you,” and then I find some reason why it can wait.\n\nIt’s silly, because I know the basement is just the basement, and the shed is just full of old paint tins and garden tools and all that clutter you swear you’ll sort one summer and never do. But what brings me here, really, is that I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I put off laundry because the machine’s down there, I avoid the garden shed, and I’ve paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo yes, someone being there might help me endure it for a minute, but it doesn’t really stop me wanting to avoid it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it’s really the spiders, more than a general thing about unpredictability. I mean, I don’t love things being out of control — who does, especially at school when the timetable changes and everyone acts like it’s a national emergency — but I don’t avoid everything like that.

What brings me in is that I’ve started arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the machine’s in the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed completely, even if I only need something small. And I’ve paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.

So yes, I suppose there’s an “unknown” bit to it, but it’s not everything unpredictable. It’s specifically where I think a spider might be.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve started arranging my whole house around not seeing spiders. It sounds silly when I say it out loud. I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but I can’t make myself go down to the basement to do laundry if I think there might be one there.\n\nAnd the garden shed—honestly, I used to keep everything organized in there, labels on boxes and all that, which my husband always teased me about. But now I avoid it, or I pay someone to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t face going into those spaces. It’s become… inconvenient, and embarrassing, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about spiders when you're not trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say constantly. I mean, if I’m at work marking papers or dealing with Year 10s being dramatic, I’m not sitting there picturing spiders all day. Though funny enough, the biology teacher has those posters with insects and things—well, not spiders technically, I suppose—and I always find myself not looking too closely.\n\nBut… no, I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts that just pop in all the time. It’s more when I’m about to go somewhere they might be, then my mind goes there. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do anything specific when you think about spiders to make yourself feel better or less anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think I have a… ritual or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’ll sometimes just back away or call my husband, but that’s more because I don’t want to deal with it.\n\nMind you, I do check corners sometimes, but then again I’ve always been a bit particular about the house. My mother was the same—she could spot dust on a skirting board from across the room, which used to drive us all mad. So I don’t know whether that’s related or just me being me.\n\nBut no, I can’t think of something specific I feel I have to do in my head to calm down. It’s more that I want to get away from the situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific actions when you think about spiders or see them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s an overwhelming urge in that way, no. I mean, if I see one, I’ll want to get away from it, obviously, or get someone else to deal with it, but I don’t have a particular action I feel I must do, like counting or touching something.\n\nAlthough, saying that, I suppose everyone has little habits, don’t they? My husband checks the back door three times before bed, and he insists that’s “just being sensible.” But with spiders, for me, it’s more… panic and wanting distance, not some specific thing I have to perform.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to avoid spiders is more about the spiders themselves or the feeling of disorder they might bring to your space?\n\nPatient: I think it’s the spiders themselves, honestly. I mean, I do like the house tidy—teaching makes you cling to order where you can, doesn’t it? My classroom cupboard is another story entirely, mind you, piles of worksheets from years ago…\n\nBut no, at home it’s more that I arrange things so I don’t have to see them. I’ll put off doing laundry if it means going down to the basement, and I avoid the garden shed unless I absolutely have to. I’ve even paid people to do silly little jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces in case there’s a spider there. So it’s not really disorder, it’s avoiding where I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is related to a fear of the spiders themselves or a fear of something else that might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s the spiders themselves, I think. Not that I genuinely believe every spider is going to do something terrible—if I’m being sensible, I know one sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.\n\nBut then I picture one dropping out of a cardboard box, or being right there by the washer when I’m reaching in, and suddenly it feels… huge, somehow. Threatening. Which is silly, I know. I’m embarrassed even saying it, because I can deal with teenagers arguing over homework, but a little spider near the laundry and I’m useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking corners is something you need to do to feel safe or in control when you think about spiders?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it needing to feel in control, exactly. I do sometimes glance around if I’m going into a room where I think there might be one, but it’s not like I have a set routine of checking every corner.\n\nCorners are funny, actually—at school the caretaker always jokes that spiders are more reliable tenants than some of the students, which doesn’t help. But no, I don’t think it’s a “must do this or I won’t be safe” sort of thing. It’s more just… if I notice myself looking, it’s because I don’t want to be surprised by one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged in a specific or symmetrical way when you're organizing your space to avoid spiders?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not symmetrical, no. I mean, I might move things around so I can see behind them, or so there aren’t dark little gaps—well, I say that and then I think of my airing cupboard, which is a disgrace, really. My sister would have a field day with it.\n\nBut no, I’m not lining things up or needing them to be even. It’s more practical, I suppose—if I’m arranging anything, it’s just so I’m less likely to get a nasty surprise. Not because it has to look a certain way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly less stressful if you didn't have to worry about spiders at all?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would, honestly. I mean, I love the house—or I did when I bought it, anyway—but it’s become harder work than it should be because I’ll put off going into certain bits of it, or I’ll leave chores because there might be spiders in the shed or behind things.\n\nAnd then I end up paying someone to do little maintenance jobs I probably ought to manage myself. Not huge things, but they add up, don’t they? My father would have said, “You’ve got a perfectly good pair of hands,” which is true, but then I’m standing there outside the garage feeling ridiculous.\n\nSo yes… if I didn’t have to worry about them, I think the house would feel a lot less stressful and probably less expensive to run.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your avoidance of spiders is making your life more orderly or controlled in any way?\n\nPatient: Orderly? No, not really. I suppose from the outside it might look like I’m “managing” things, but it doesn’t feel controlled. It feels like the house is sort of dictating where I will and won’t go, which is ridiculous because it’s my house.\n\nI’ll put off laundry if it means going down to the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole… production in my head before I even open the door. And then I end up paying someone to do small jobs I know how to do myself, just because I can’t quite make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo no, it’s not making life more orderly. If anything it makes everything more awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are avoiding spiders because you are afraid of what might happen if you see one, or because you feel like you need to avoid them to follow a certain rule or pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I’m afraid of what’ll happen if I see one, not because of a rule or pattern. I don’t have a system I’m following.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve ended up arranging my own house around not seeing spiders, which sounds mad when I say it. I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down to the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed altogether. And then I’ll pay someone to do some small job I know perfectly well how to do, just because I can’t make myself go into that space.\n\nIt’s not like, “I must do this in this order.” It’s more, “I can’t face opening that door in case there’s one there.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders would decrease if you felt more in control of your environment?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yes… if I actually felt in control of the places spiders might be, I’d probably avoid them less. But the trouble is, it’s my own house, and I still don’t feel like I can just go where I need to go.\n\nI keep sort of arranging things around not seeing them. Like I’ll put off doing laundry because it means going down into the basement, and the garden shed becomes this whole thing in my mind before I even open the door. It’s silly, because I’m a teacher, I can manage a classroom full of teenagers, but then a shed defeats me.\n\nAnd I’ve paid people to do small jobs that I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces. So yes, I suppose if I felt more in control, it might help… but at the moment it doesn’t feel very controllable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid spiders if you knew for certain that they would not harm you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, I probably still would, which is the embarrassing bit. I do know, rationally, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to harm me. I can say that quite calmly sitting here.\n\nBut if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer when I go to do laundry, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not in a sensible way. It’s like my body reacts before my brain has had a chance to be reasonable.\n\nIt’s funny, because I’ll teach students about checking facts and not jumping to conclusions, and then I’m defeated by something the size of a coin. But yes… even if I knew it wouldn’t hurt me, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something you feel you must do, or is it more that you're trying to avoid the discomfort they cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the discomfort, I think. I don’t feel like there’s a rule saying I must avoid them, if that makes sense. It’s just that when I imagine going into the basement or opening the garden shed, I get this horrible feeling and then I start working around it.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in, I suppose — I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the washer’s in the basement, and the shed just becomes… no, I’ll deal with that later. And then later becomes weeks. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, which is ridiculous, but at the time it feels easier than making myself go in there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would avoid other small creatures, like mice or insects, in a similar way if you thought they might be in the same places as the spiders?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no… I mean, I don’t love the idea of mice or insects either, but spiders are the thing I arrange everything around.\n\nAlthough, funny enough, our old science block at school had mice once, and everyone made such a fuss, and I was the one saying, “Just put the food away properly,” so I can be sensible about other creatures. But at home, with spiders, it becomes different.\n\nThat’s what’s got ridiculous, really. I put off doing laundry because the machine’s in the basement, and I avoid the garden shed like it’s some huge ordeal. I’ve even paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I think spiders might be.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding spiders more about reducing your anxiety or specifically about not wanting to see the spiders themselves?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s both, but more specifically I’m trying not to see them. The anxiety is part of it, obviously, but it’s the thought of actually opening the basement door or going into the shed and there being one right there that makes me start reorganising everything.\n\nI mean, it sounds daft when I say it out loud. I’ve got piles of laundry I’ll put off because the washer’s in the basement, and then I’ll busy myself with marking or cleaning the kitchen instead, as if that’s more urgent. And the garden shed… I just avoid it. I’ve even paid people to do small jobs I know perfectly well how to do, just so I don’t have to go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the need to avoid other places or situations if you couldn't control whether or not spiders were present, even if spiders weren't actually there?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think if I felt I couldn’t control whether they might be there, I’d start avoiding it. It’s not even always that I’ve seen one — it’s the possibility.\n\nThat’s really what’s been happening at home. I’ve got my own little routes and habits now, which sounds absurd. The basement, for instance, because the washer’s down there, I’ll put off laundry until it’s ridiculous. And the garden shed — I’ll need something from it and then decide I don’t really need it after all. I’ve even paid someone to do small jobs I could do myself, just because it meant I didn’t have to go into those spaces.\n\nSorry, I’m going round the houses a bit, but yes — if I can’t feel sure there won’t be spiders, I tend to avoid the place.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still avoid those places, like the basement or the garden shed, if you were accompanied by someone you trust?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit less, if someone was with me… but I think I’d still try to get out of it, honestly. I’ve even had times where my husband says, “I’ll come down with you,” and then I find some reason why it can wait.\n\nIt’s silly, because I know the basement is just the basement, and the shed is just full of old paint tins and garden tools and all that clutter you swear you’ll sort one summer and never do. But what brings me here, really, is that I keep arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I put off laundry because the machine’s down there, I avoid the garden shed, and I’ve paid people to do little jobs I could perfectly well do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo yes, someone being there might help me endure it for a minute, but it doesn’t really stop me wanting to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your avoidance of spiders is something that is specifically about the spiders, or is it part of a larger pattern of avoiding things that might be unpredictable or out of your control?\n\nPatient: I think it’s really the spiders, more than a general thing about unpredictability. I mean, I don’t love things being out of control — who does, especially at school when the timetable changes and everyone acts like it’s a national emergency — but I don’t avoid everything like that.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I’ve started arranging my own house around not seeing spiders. I’ll put off laundry because the machine’s in the basement, or I’ll avoid the garden shed completely, even if I only need something small. And I’ve paid people to do little jobs I know perfectly well how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces.\n\nSo yes, I suppose there’s an “unknown” bit to it, but it’s not everything unpredictable. It’s specifically where I think a spider might be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
